            where <span class="inline-formula"><i>E</i><sub>reservoir</sub></span>Â is the reservoir evaporation rateÂ (<span class="inline-formula">mmâd<sup>â1</sup></span>); <span class="inline-formula">Î</span>Â is the slope of the saturation vapor pressure
curveÂ (<span class="inline-formula"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M22" display="inline" overflow="scroll" dspmath="mathml"><mrow class="unit"><mi mathvariant="normal">kPa</mi><mspace width="0.125em" linebreak="nobreak"></mspace><msup><mi></mi><mo>â</mo></msup><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">C</mi><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn mathvariant="normal">1</mn></mrow></msup></mrow></math><span><svg xmlns:svg="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" width="44pt" height="13pt" class="hide-js svg-formula" dspmath="mathimg" md5hash="134f434b0ca5632c4fabf1080c13481d"><image xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-ie00002.svg" width="100%" height="13pt" src="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-ie00002.png"></image></svg></span></span>); <span class="inline-formula"><i>R</i><sub>n</sub></span>Â is the net radiation (<span class="inline-formula"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M24" display="inline" overflow="scroll" dspmath="mathml"><mrow class="unit"><mi mathvariant="normal">MJ</mi><mspace width="0.125em" linebreak="nobreak"></mspace><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">m</mi><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn mathvariant="normal">2</mn></mrow></msup><mspace linebreak="nobreak" width="0.125em"></mspace><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn mathvariant="normal">1</mn></mrow></msup></mrow></math><span><svg xmlns:svg="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" width="55pt" height="13pt" class="hide-js svg-formula" dspmath="mathimg" md5hash="3c0221226835a6d2772ceafe445afa1d"><image xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-ie00003.svg" width="100%" height="13pt" src="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-ie00003.png"></image></svg></span></span>); <span class="inline-formula"><i>Î³</i></span>Â is the psychrometric constant
(<span class="inline-formula"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M26" display="inline" overflow="scroll" dspmath="mathml"><mrow class="unit"><mi mathvariant="normal">kPa</mi><mspace width="0.125em" linebreak="nobreak"></mspace><msup><mi></mi><mo>â</mo></msup><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">C</mi><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn mathvariant="normal">1</mn></mrow></msup></mrow></math><span><svg xmlns:svg="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" width="44pt" height="13pt" class="hide-js svg-formula" dspmath="mathimg" md5hash="3d37992069f97a90f1ebca906e058bf4"><image xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-ie00004.svg" width="100%" height="13pt" src="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-ie00004.png"></image></svg></span></span>); <span class="inline-formula"><i>Î»</i></span>Â is the latent heat of vaporization (<span class="inline-formula">MJâkg<sup>â1</sup></span>); <span class="inline-formula"><i>u</i><sub>2</sub></span>Â is the wind speed at 2â<span class="inline-formula">m</span>Â height
(<span class="inline-formula">mâs<sup>â1</sup></span>);
<span class="inline-formula"><i>L</i><sub>f</sub></span>Â is the monthly reservoir fetch lengthÂ (<span class="inline-formula">m</span>); <span class="inline-formula"><i>e</i><sub>s</sub></span>Â and <span class="inline-formula"><i>e</i><sub>a</sub></span>Â are the saturated vapor pressure at air temperature
and the air vapor pressureÂ (<span class="inline-formula">kPa</span>), respectively; <span class="inline-formula"><i>Ï</i><sub>w</sub></span>Â is the density of waterÂ (<span class="inline-formula">kgâm<sup>â3</sup></span>); <span class="inline-formula"><i>c</i><sub>w</sub></span>Â is the specific
heat of waterÂ (<span class="inline-formula"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M40" display="inline" overflow="scroll" dspmath="mathml"><mrow class="unit"><mi mathvariant="normal">MJ</mi><mspace width="0.125em" linebreak="nobreak"></mspace><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">kg</mi><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn mathvariant="normal">1</mn></mrow></msup><mspace linebreak="nobreak" width="0.125em"></mspace><msup><mi></mi><mo>â</mo></msup><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">C</mi><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn mathvariant="normal">1</mn></mrow></msup></mrow></math><span><svg xmlns:svg="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" width="63pt" height="15pt" class="hide-js svg-formula" dspmath="mathimg" md5hash="4447c6fc592822f983f1dce43f65a8c9"><image xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-ie00005.svg" width="100%" height="15pt" src="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-ie00005.png"></image></svg></span></span>); <span class="inline-formula"><i>h</i></span>Â is the average water depthÂ (<span class="inline-formula">m</span>); <span class="inline-formula"><i>T</i><sub>w</sub></span>Â and <span class="inline-formula"><i>T</i><sub>w0</sub></span>Â are the water
column temperature at the current time step and at the previous time stepÂ (<span class="inline-formula"><sup>â</sup>C</span>), respectively; and <span class="inline-formula">Î<i>t</i></span>Â is the time step (set
asÂ 1Â month in this study).</p>
      <p id="d1e1173">It is worth noting that this algorithm does not need parameter calibration and only requires <span class="inline-formula"><i>L</i><sub>f</sub></span>,Â <span class="inline-formula"><i>h</i></span>, and four meteorological
variables (air temperature, <span class="inline-formula"><sup>â</sup>C</span>; wind speed, <span class="inline-formula">mâs<sup>â1</sup></span>; vapor pressure deficit, <span class="inline-formula">kPa</span>; and surface downward shortwave
radiation, <span class="inline-formula"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="M52" display="inline" overflow="scroll" dspmath="mathml"><mrow class="unit"><mi mathvariant="normal">MJ</mi><mspace linebreak="nobreak" width="0.125em"></mspace><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">m</mi><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn mathvariant="normal">2</mn></mrow></msup><mspace width="0.125em" linebreak="nobreak"></mspace><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn mathvariant="normal">1</mn></mrow></msup></mrow></math><span><svg xmlns:svg="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" width="55pt" height="13pt" class="hide-js svg-formula" dspmath="mathimg" md5hash="5a4f0cc1765f9a1f3e9b48d8af3f3b4c"><image xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-ie00006.svg" width="100%" height="13pt" src="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-ie00006.png"></image></svg></span></span>) for the evaporation rate calculation. In previous
studies (Zhao etÂ al., 2022; Tian etÂ al., 2022), the TerraClimate dataset (Abatzoglou etÂ al., 2018) has been shown to be the most appropriate
meteorological dataset for reliable estimates of reservoir evaporation rates compared to other global datasets. Thus, we adopted the TerraClimate to
generate meteorological data time series by averaging gridded forcing data within reservoir shapefiles. Monthly reservoir fetch values, <span class="inline-formula"><i>L</i><sub>f</sub></span>,
were calculated using (1)Â reservoir shapefiles, (2)Â wind direction data from the NCEP (National Centers for Environmental Prediction), and (3)Â water
surface area time series. The average reservoir water depths, <span class="inline-formula"><i>h</i></span>, are taken from the GRanD and HydroLAKES datasets. For reservoirs not recorded in
these two datasets, we determined their water depths from their water surface areas through the areaâdepth curves fitted to all available data. A
detailed algorithm flowchart, all equations, and examples are provided in Fig.Â S3 and in Zhao and Gao (2019).</p>
</div><div class="sec"><h2 id="Ch1.S2.SS5"><span class="label">2.5</span>âData and methodology for generating reservoir catchment boundaries</h2>
  
      <p id="d1e1284">In this study, two types of reservoir upstream catchment boundaries (hereafter referred to as catchment) are defined: full catchments and
intermediate catchments (Fig.Â 2). The full catchment is defined as a reservoir's full upstream contributing area, whereas the intermediate catchment
is determined by subtracting the area contributed by upstream reservoirs from the catchment area of the current reservoir. Obviously, full catchments
are independent of each other, but for reservoirs with larger catchments, they can lead to a significant loss of information such as the local
features and variability. Thus, intermediate catchments, which are part of the large river networks, can complement this information, and<span id="page2787"></span> this ensures more
representative and accurate mapping of local variables.</p>
      <p id="d1e1287">To identify catchment boundaries (Fig.Â A2), we used an automatic outlet relocation algorithm (Xie etÂ al., 2022) to automatically delineate a large
number of catchments. This algorithm can correct the river networks by analyzing the gradients of flow accumulations along the rivers and can rapidly
delineate catchments (Xie etÂ al., 2022). More importantly, it has been intensively validated for 1398Â catchments of varying size and geographic
regions, showing that 94.1â% of catchments were correctly delineated. This algorithm requires the flow directions and gauge locations as input. In this
study, we used flow directions from MERIT Hydro and dam locations from the GeoDAR dataset. MERIT Hydro is a new global hydrography map that has a
fine resolution ofÂ 90â<span class="inline-formula">m</span> and shows good performance in terms of river basin shape and flow accumulation area. This algorithm can generate the
full catchments of each reservoir, and we employed some additional operations to remove topology errors and obtain intermediate catchments. Firstly,
we cleared the holes to remove topology errors across full catchments. Secondly, we checked the full catchments and removed the unrealistic or
incorrect catchments. Thirdly, we generated intermediate catchments by removing the overlapping areas of upstream reservoirs from the full catchment
of the current reservoir using QGISÂ 3.24. Lastly, we fixed the invalid geometry of intermediate catchments by eliminating geometry errors (TextÂ S1 in
the Supplement).</p>
</div><div class="sec"><h2 id="Ch1.S2.SS6"><span class="label">2.6</span>âData and methodology for generating catchment-level characteristics</h2>
  
      <p id="d1e1306">Catchment attributes can be categorized into six types: reservoir and catchment body characteristics, climate, geology and soil, topography, land
cover, and anthropogenic activity characteristics. The sources for different datasets were chosen to ensure use of high-quality data, the most reputable sources,
global coverage, theoretical impacts on reservoirs, and high spatial resolution as far as possible. For example, the NSCD versionÂ 3 (National
Station-based Climatic DataÂ set) is the most widely used in situ meteorological dataset in China, while MERIT DEM is a widely used global hydrography map. Here, we give a brief description of data processing, while the detailed interpretations, uncertainties, and limitations of source
datasets are available in Sect.Â 3, the Supplement, and Fig.Â A3 in the Appendix.</p>
      <p id="d1e1309">Some necessary data format conversions (e.g., netCDF to raster format) and reprojections are firstly conducted for related attribute datasets. Then,
we used different methods to calculate catchment-level characteristics from raster and vector data. Statistics values were calculated for raster grids
within each catchment. In the case of continuous variables such as temperature and elevation, we calculated their mean, maximum, minimum, and range
values. For categorical variables such as geological maps and land use features, we calculated the percentages of each variable and determined their
dominant type. The implementation for processing raster data is done in the local Python and RÂ environments (package: rasterstats) and the GEE
platform. For vectorial data such as the rivers and catchments in ESRI shapefile format, the shape features of each catchment are calculated using the
local Python scripts, and other catchment attributes like stream density are determined by the ratio of the intersection area between the vectorial
data and the catchment extent layer to the total catchment area. After repeating these procedures for each catchment, catchment-level attributes are
prepared for both full catchments and intermediate catchments.</p>

<span class="tableCitations"></span><div class="table-wrap" id="Ch1.T1"><div class="caption"><p id="d1e1315"><strong class="caption-number">TableÂ 1</strong>Summary of the data provided in Res-CN.</p></div><a class="table-link" href="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-t01.png" target="_blank"><img src="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-t01-thumb.png" target="_blank" data-webversion="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-t01-web.png" data-width="2067" data-height="1406" data-printversion="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-t01.png" data-csvversion="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-t01.xlsx"></a><p class="downloads"><a class="triangle journal-contentLinkColor table-download" href="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-t01.png" target="_blank">Download Print Version</a><span class="hide-on-mobile download-separator"> | </span><a class="triangle journal-contentLinkColor table-download" href="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-t01.xlsx" target="_blank">Download XLSX</a></p></div>

<div class="section3-content show-no-js hide-on-mobile-soft"><div class="sec"><h2 id="Ch1.S3.SS1"><span class="label">3.1</span>âDescription of the Res-CN database</h2>
  
      <p id="d1e1645">We here provide summarized information on the components of Res-CN in TableÂ 1. Detailed descriptions of each component are shown in the
following sections and in the Supplement. In this study, we constructed reservoir-catchment characteristics for 3254Â reservoirs recorded in the
GeoDAR database (Wang etÂ al., 2022) with water surface areas ranging fromÂ 0.004 to 1373.77â<span class="inline-formula">km<sup>2</sup></span> (Fig.Â S4) and storage capacity totaling 682â595â<span class="inline-formula">km<sup>3</sup></span> (73.2â% of reservoir water storage capacity in China). Using
both full catchments and intermediate catchments, characteristics of reservoir catchments were extracted, with a total of 512Â attributes in six
categories (TableÂ 1). Besides, time series of reservoir states such as water level and water surface area are also provided, with their comprehensive
evaluation reports (i.e., statistics and figures in PDF and Excel files) based on in situ data when available. For more details, please refer to the
data repository and the following sections.</p>

      <div class="fig" id="Ch1.F3"><a target="_blank" class="figure-link" href="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-f03-web.png"><img alt="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-f03" data-webversion="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-f03-web.png" src="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-f03-thumb.png" data-width="2067" data-height="1043"></a><div class="caption"><p id="d1e1672"><strong class="caption-number">FigureÂ 3</strong>Distribution of the delineated catchments (intermediate catchments and full catchments). Each category's histogram indicates the number of basins (out ofÂ 3254). In a histogram, the <span class="inline-formula"><i>x</i></span>Â axis represents the number of basins, while the <span class="inline-formula"><i>y</i></span>Â axis represents each subplot's caption. Circle sizes are proportional to catchment areas.</p></div><p class="downloads"></p></div>

      <div class="fig" id="Ch1.F4"><a target="_blank" class="figure-link" href="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-f04-web.png"><img alt="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-f04" data-webversion="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-f04-web.png" src="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-f04-thumb.png" data-width="2067" data-height="1737"></a><div class="caption"><p id="d1e1697"><strong class="caption-number">FigureÂ 4</strong>Data availability of the altimetric reservoir water level time seriesÂ <strong>(a)</strong> and number of satellites for high-rate productsÂ <strong>(b)</strong> as well as some examples to illustrate time series of standard-rate products over nine selected reservoirsÂ <strong>(câk)</strong>.</p></div><p class="downloads"></p></div>

</div><div class="sec"><h2 id="Ch1.S3.SS2"><span class="label">3.2</span>âRes-CN products for the delineated catchment characteristics</h2>
  
      <p id="d1e1723">Res-CN provides 3254Â full catchments and 435Â intermediate catchments (Fig.Â 3). The median catchment size of full catchments is 294â<span class="inline-formula">km<sup>2</sup></span>, with
a range ofÂ 0.94 to 981â473â<span class="inline-formula">km<sup>2</sup></span>. The plausibility of full-catchment delineation was assessed by comparing the area of the delineated
catchments with the areas of two declared references: GRanD (Lehner etÂ al., 2011) and LakeATLAS (Lehner etÂ al., 2022). LakeATLAS delineated upstream
drainage area of more than 1.4Â million lakes and reservoirs globally based on the lake pour points and the 15â<span class="inline-formula">arcsec</span>Â drainage direction
grids of HydroSHEDS. A similar approach was applied in GRanD to estimate the areas of upstream catchments over 7320Â reservoirs globally. To
compare Res-CN with GRanD and LakeATLAS, we spatially joined reservoir shapefiles from both datasets, matching reservoirs that overlapped for greater
thanÂ 90â% of their extent. Based on this subset of reservoirs, we found that catchment areas delineated in this study corresponded relatively well
to catchment areas in both GRanD (CCâ<span class="inline-formula">=</span>â0.99, <span class="inline-formula"><i>n</i></span>â<span class="inline-formula">=</span>â910) and LakeATLAS (CCâ<span class="inline-formula">=</span>â0.91, <span class="inline-formula"><i>n</i></span>â<span class="inline-formula">=</span>â2147), which proves the<span id="page2788"></span> reliability of our
delineated catchments. Large discrepancies occur in 55Â catchments, whose absolute relative error is greater thanÂ 100â% (Fig.Â 3e andÂ f). Small
reservoirs located near confluences between rivers of different sizes are more likely to be affected by this issue, as a minor spatial mismatch can
assign a reservoir to the small catchment of the tributary stream rather than the large catchment of the mainstream, and vice versa (Fig.Â S5). The differences in catchment delineation between these datasets result from differences
in both DEM and methods for flow direction correction and depression filling and pour point correction. In this study, the widely verified MERIT
Hydro flow directions atÂ 3â<span class="inline-formula">arcsec</span> are used, and we suggest that caution should be taken when using catchments with large error
discrepancies with LakeATLAS, which is based on the 15â<span class="inline-formula">arcsec</span>Â drainage direction grids of HydroSHEDS (Fig.Â S5a). Intermediate catchments
provide information regarding the variability of local features and the upstreamâdownstream relationship. The median catchment size of intermediate
catchments is 936â<span class="inline-formula">km<sup>2</sup></span>, with a rangeÂ ofÂ 1 to 279â424â<span class="inline-formula">km<sup>2</sup></span>.</p>

      <div class="fig" id="Ch1.F5"><a target="_blank" class="figure-link" href="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-f05-web.png"><img alt="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-f05" data-webversion="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-f05-web.png" src="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-f05-thumb.png" data-width="1963" data-height="1891"></a><div class="caption"><p id="d1e1840"><strong class="caption-number">FigureÂ 5</strong>High-rate water level time series over reservoirs Xinfengjiang (264â<span class="inline-formula">km<sup>2</sup></span>) from Sentinel-3A, SARAL/AltiKa, Sentinel-3B, CryoSat-2 and ICESat-2; Three Gorges (852â<span class="inline-formula">km<sup>2</sup></span>) from Sentinel-3A, SARAL/AltiKa, Sentinel-3B, CryoSat-2, ICESat-2, and Jason-3; Guanting (90â<span class="inline-formula">km<sup>2</sup></span>) from Jason-3, CryoSat-2, ICESat-2, Sentinel-3A, and Sentinel-3B; Ertan (65â<span class="inline-formula">km<sup>2</sup></span>) from Jason-3, SARAL/AltiKa, ICESat-2, Sentinel-3A, and Sentinel-3B; and Longyangxia (285â<span class="inline-formula">km<sup>2</sup></span>) from SARAL/AltiKa, CryoSat-2, ICESat-2, and Sentinel-3B.</p></div><p class="downloads"></p></div>

</div><div class="sec"><h2 id="Ch1.S3.SS3"><span class="label">3.3</span>âRes-CN products for time series of reservoir states</h2>
  
<div class="sec"><h3 id="Ch1.S3.SS3.SSS1"><span class="label">3.3.1</span>âReservoir water level</h3>
  
      <p id="d1e1919">There are two modes of reservoir water level time series available from Res-CN: SR and HR. FigureÂ 4 demonstrates their spatial coverages, data source
and availability, and evaluation reports against in situ observations. Among them, over 200Â reservoirs are visited by CryoSat-2, SARAL/AltiKa, and
ICESat-2, and onlyÂ 192 and 54Â reservoirs are covered by Sentinel-3A and Jason-3, respectively (TableÂ 1). Data quality was generally good for smaller
RMSE values (<span class="inline-formula">&lt;</span>â0.3â<span class="inline-formula">m</span>), moderate for those betweenÂ 0.3 andÂ 1.0â<span class="inline-formula">m</span>, and relatively poor for those greater thanÂ 1.0â<span class="inline-formula">m</span>. For each
altimeter and HR product, the percentages of validated reservoirs with good, moderate, and poor data quality areÂ 44â%, 33â%, and 23â% (Sentinel-3A:
validated in 34Â reservoirs); 55â%, 18â%, and 27â% (Sentinel-3B:Â 22); 38â%, 37â%, and 25â% (SARAL/AltiKa:Â 8); 71â%, 10â%, and
19â% (ICESat-2:Â 31); 50â%, 36â%, and 14â% (Jason-3:Â 14); 22â%, 56â%, and 22â% (CryoSat-2:Â 27); and 25â%, 73â%, and 2â% (HR
products:Â 84). We found that in most cases there is no notable difference in terms of RMSE values between different retracking algorithms (Fig.Â S6). It should be noted that multisource<span id="page2789"></span> altimetric measurements are merged for a
specific reservoir by using the SRÂ time series with the lowest RMSE from the retracking algorithm. FigureÂ 5 shows examples of the HR products over a
sample of six reservoirs with different areas. Single-satellite altimetric time series capture reservoir water level dynamics well, leading to improved temporal
resolution of the HRÂ product. A cross-validation of the time series against other existing databases as well as a comparison of their spatial coverage in
China (TableÂ S1 and Fig.Â S7) further demonstrated the advantages of our
products. Water levels provided by Res-CN generally agree with those provided by existing similar products (Hydroweb, G-REALM, and DAHITI) with
CCÂ values exceedingÂ 0.9, although there are some discrepancies. As an example, Res-CN time series are much denser and less noisy than Hydroweb's in
most reservoirs. At the Sanhezha reservoir, G-REALM failed to capture the clear fluctuation pattern, while large discrepancies were apparent at the
Fengman reservoir in 2020 between Res-CN and Hydroweb (Fig.Â S7).</p>

      <div class="fig" id="Ch1.F6"><a target="_blank" class="figure-link" href="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-f06-web.png"><img alt="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-f06" data-webversion="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-f06-web.png" src="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-f06-thumb.png" data-printversion="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-f06.jpg" data-width="2067" data-height="1142"></a><div class="caption"><p id="d1e1955"><strong class="caption-number">FigureÂ 6</strong>Cross-validation of reconstructed monthly reservoir area values by comparing to two existing global datasets. (<strong>a</strong>, <strong>c</strong>,Â and <strong>d</strong>)Â Comparison between reconstructed monthly reservoir areas and GRSAD. (<strong>b</strong>, <strong>e</strong>,Â and <strong>f</strong>)Â Comparison between reconstructed monthly reservoir areas and RealSAT.</p></div><p class="downloads"><a class="triangle journal-contentLinkColor figure-download" href="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-f06.jpg" target="_blank">Download</a></p></div>

      <p id="d1e1983">We should consider some limitations for further improvements despite our products' good performance and expanded spatial coverage. We provided the
uncertainty information for each value of the time series in the data product file. The SD (standard deviation) estimates can quantify the accuracy of
the water level along the track at the level of individual data points (Fig.Â S8). Water level time series for each reservoir are available in Rec-CN as Excel files, PDF files, and detailed evaluation reports based on in situ
data when available (see âData availabilityâ section and Fig.Â S9). In our study,
more than 80â% of reservoirs with inadequate altimetric measurements are removed due to the inherent limitations of satellite altimeters. There
are still challenges to delivering useful measurements for certain reservoirs along the Yangtze and Yellow rivers, and data quality is poor in terms
of RMSE values regardless of reservoir size. It may be possible to apply advanced algorithms, such as machine learning, in future studies to achieve
better performance regardless of whether reservoirs represent different behaviors.</p>
</div>
<div class="sec"><h3 id="Ch1.S3.SS3.SSS2"><span class="label">3.3.2</span>âReservoir water surface area</h3>
  
      <p id="d1e1994">Res-CN provides monthly reservoir water surface area data derived from Landsat and Sentinel-2 images during 1984â2021, along with their detailed
evaluation reports (see âData availabilityâ section). We compare these datasets with in situ water levels and altimetric measurements as well as
other areal datasets (GRSAD and RealSAT). RealSAT generated 681â137 monthly lake surface area maps from Landsat images during 1984â2015 using an
ORBIT (ordering-based information transfer) approach that has been validated on 94Â large reservoirs. As opposed to RealSAT, which generated new static
lake polygons from water occurrence data, GRSAD used existing static surface water polygons, HydroLAKES and GRanD, to create monthly areas for
6817Â global reservoirs based on Landsat images over the last 35Â years. The 139Â reservoirs with daily inÂ situ observations generally show good
agreement in terms of reservoir area and in situ water level time series withÂ 81â% having CC values higher<span id="page2790"></span> thanÂ 0.5. The CCÂ value is expected to
decrease for reservoirs with small areal sizes or steep banks. As an example, the CCÂ values of Gutian and Hengjiang reservoirsÂ (10 andÂ 2â<span class="inline-formula">km<sup>2</sup></span>)
areÂ 0.37 andÂ 0.06, respectively. There is also a high median CCÂ value ofÂ 0.70 in Res-CN water surface area against altimetric water level time
series. As compared to HR altimetric water levels, approximatelyÂ 63â% of the 244Â compared reservoirs have good CCÂ values exceedingÂ 0.5, includingÂ 69
with CCÂ reservoirs valuesÂ <span class="inline-formula">&gt;</span>â0.8. For SRÂ altimetric water levels, approximatelyÂ 63â% of the compared 557Â reservoirs have good CCÂ values
exceedingÂ 0.5, among which 212Â reservoirs show very good agreement with CCÂ valuesÂ <span class="inline-formula">&gt;</span>â0.8. To compare Res-CN with these two datasets, we spatially
joined reservoir polygons from all datasets, identifying reservoirs with more thanÂ 90â% overlap. The subset of reservoirs shows good agreement
with GRSAD (Fig.Â 6a, median CCÂ value ofÂ 0.65, rBIASâ<span class="inline-formula">=</span>â<span class="inline-formula">â</span>10â%, rRMSEâ<span class="inline-formula">=</span>â22â%, <span class="inline-formula"><i>n</i></span>â<span class="inline-formula">=</span>â488) and RealSAT (Fig.Â 6b, median
CCÂ valueâ<span class="inline-formula">=</span>â0.64, rBIASâ<span class="inline-formula">=</span>â<span class="inline-formula">â</span>5â%, rRMSEâ<span class="inline-formula">=</span>â20â%, <span class="inline-formula"><i>n</i></span>â<span class="inline-formula">=</span>â288). Since RealSAT and our collected in situ observations do not
overlap, we validated only the GRSAD datasets against in situ water levels. GRSAD also showed a good agreement with CC values higher thanÂ 0.5
forÂ 58â% of the 139Â reservoirs, including 38Â reservoirs that showed a very good agreement with CCÂ valuesÂ <span class="inline-formula">&gt;</span>â0.8. In sum, these comparisons suggest
that our dataset is reliable.</p>
      <p id="d1e2108">Uncertainties in surface water area estimates are generally attributed to satellite images and algorithms. As reported by Zhao etÂ al. (2022), the
uncertainty of the Landsat-based GRSAD areal dataset isÂ 6.1â%. In this study, we generated a more reliable reservoir water area product by fusing both
Landsat and Sentinel-2 images (Fig.Â S10), using an algorithm that can largely
reduce the impacts of cloud<span id="page2791"></span> contamination (Donchyts etÂ al., 2022). There is strong evidence to suggest that this algorithm performs well in this
regard, as it has been widely validated in 768 reservoirs of different sizes and climate zones located in Spain, India, South Africa, and the USA
(Donchyts etÂ al., 2022). Nevertheless, some limitations and future developments should be considered. Our first option is to use Sentinel-1 data to
provide more information in cloudy regions. Furthermore, the algorithm may be improved by either the multiclass Otsu algorithm or using advanced machine learning
methods.</p>

      <div class="fig" id="Ch1.F7"><a target="_blank" class="figure-link" href="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-f07-web.png"><img alt="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-f07" data-webversion="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-f07-web.png" src="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-f07-thumb.png" data-width="2067" data-height="1764"></a><div class="caption"><p id="d1e2113"><strong class="caption-number">FigureÂ 7</strong>Time series of water surface area and storage anomaly in selected reservoirs. RMSEÂ (<span class="inline-formula">km<sup>3</sup></span>), NRMSE, and CCÂ values are given at the top of each subplot when in situ observations are available. Note that time series of water surface area and storage anomaly of the remaining reservoirs are available in our datasets.</p></div><p class="downloads"></p></div>

</div>
<div class="sec"><h3 id="Ch1.S3.SS3.SSS3"><span class="label">3.3.3</span>âReservoir storage anomaly</h3>
  
      <p id="d1e2141">The Res-CN database provides monthly reservoir water storage anomaly for 3254 Chinese reservoirs during 1984â2021 using the DEM's areaâstorage model,
along with their detailed evaluation reports (see âData availabilityâ section). Seventy-four percent of reservoirs (89Â of 119Â validated
reservoirs with in situ data) have good data quality with a RMSE value belowÂ 0.2â<span class="inline-formula">km<sup>3</sup></span> and a NRMSE value belowÂ 30â%. NRMSE, CC, and RMSE
have median values ofÂ 21â%, 0.53, andÂ 0.03â<span class="inline-formula">km<sup>3</sup></span>, respectively. The lowest NRMSE isÂ 7â% in the Luhun reservoir, which has a high CCÂ value
ofÂ 0.90 and low RMSE value ofÂ 0.016â<span class="inline-formula">km<sup>3</sup></span>. FigureÂ 7 shows variations in water storage in small, medium, and large reservoirs located<span id="page2792"></span> in
different climate zones. The remotely sensed storage anomalies generally agree with the observations represented by the statistical metrics, although
some large discrepancies occur in peak values. We find that our error statistics in terms of NRMSE are a bit higher than previous studies in terms of
NRMSE belowÂ 20â% (Zhong et al., 2020). The errors result from the inaccuracy of the areaâstorage
model developed by the DEM as well as the error of water surface areas at certain reservoirs. To solve this problem, we provide another type of storage
variation estimate for 335Â reservoirs using satellite water surface areas and water levels (see Sect.Â 2.3; Shen etÂ al., 2022a). The accuracy of
storage anomalies is improved, with median statistics ofÂ CC, NRMSE, and RMSE ofÂ 0.89, 11â%, andÂ 0.021â<span class="inline-formula">km<sup>3</sup></span>, respectively.</p>
      <p id="d1e2188">The uncertainties in storage anomalies are primarily attributed to three sources, i.e., the altimetric water level, water surface area estimations
from Landsat and Sentinel-2 images, and the error resulting from their combination (the hypsometric curve). FigureÂ S11 provides an example that illustrates how the uncertainties in satellite datasets propagate
to storage anomalies. According to Shen etÂ al. (2022b), the primary source of error in storage anomaly is water surface area and the hypsometric
curve. Regarding the water surface area, after applying the algorithm developed by Donchyts etÂ al. (2022), these errors and impacts can be reduced to
a large extent. Meanwhile, we employed five hypsometric relationships, and we used the one with the highest <span class="inline-formula"><i>R</i><sup>2</sup></span>Â value for further use. For more
thanÂ 80â% of reservoirs, the <span class="inline-formula"><i>R</i><sup>2</sup></span>Â values are greater thanÂ 0.5, providing a strong foundation for storage anomaly estimates. Nonetheless, the current
satellite sensors have limitations, as evidenced by the significant discrepancies observed in peak values (Fig.Â 7). The increasing temporal resolution
and data accuracy of satellite datasets, such as the SWOT (Surface Water and Ocean Topography) mission, will likely improve the accuracy of storage anomaly estimates in the future.</p>

      <div class="fig" id="Ch1.F8"><a target="_blank" class="figure-link" href="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-f08-web.png"><img alt="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-f08" data-webversion="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-f08-web.png" src="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-f08-thumb.png" data-width="2067" data-height="590"></a><div class="caption"><p id="d1e2215"><strong class="caption-number">FigureÂ 8</strong>Validation of reconstructed monthly reservoir evaporation values. <strong>(a)</strong>Â Long-term mean evaporation rates and <strong>(b)</strong>Â water surface areas during 1984â2021.</p></div><p class="downloads"></p></div>

</div>
<div class="sec"><h3 id="Ch1.S3.SS3.SSS4"><span class="label">3.3.4</span>âReservoir evaporation</h3>
  
      <p id="d1e2238"><span id="page2794"></span>Res-CN provides monthly reservoir evaporation values for 3254 Chinese reservoirs during 1984â2021. Detailed validations of the algorithm can be found
in Zhao and Gao (2019, Zhao et al., 2022) and Tian etÂ al. (2021). The validation of simulated evaporation at an
annual scale from Tian etÂ al. (2022) at 47Â reservoirs was summarized in Fig.Â S12
through a literature review. The results in Fig.Â S12 indicate that the modeled average annual evaporation rates match well with the observed
rates. Specifically, the percent bias (PBIAS), NashâSutcliffe efficiency (NSE), and root-mean-square error (RMSE) were found to beÂ 0.02â%, 0.82,
andÂ 11.2â<span class="inline-formula">mm</span>, respectively. This high level of agreement suggests that the Penman method is a reliable approach for calculating reservoir
evaporation rates in China. FigureÂ S13 shows the long-term mean meteorological
variables that were used to calculate the evaporation rates. We found that reservoirs located in the southern and coastal areas have significantly
larger values than other areas due to larger radiation values. For example, the mean evaporation for 613Â reservoirs in the Pearl River basin is
1210â<span class="inline-formula">mmâyr<sup>â1</sup></span>,
while the mean evaporation for 26Â reservoirs in the Songhua River basin is 717â<span class="inline-formula">mmâyr<sup>â1</sup></span> (Fig.Â 8a). With respect to the mean reservoir areas
(Fig.Â 8b), small and medium reservoirs are widely distributed across the nation. The CC values between the mean evaporation rates and the surface
shortwave radiation, vapor pressure deficit, mean air temperature, and wind speed areÂ 0.88, 0.84, 0.86, andÂ 0.88, respectively.</p>

      <div class="fig" id="Ch1.F9"><a target="_blank" class="figure-link" href="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-f09-web.png"><img alt="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-f09" data-webversion="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-f09-web.png" src="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-f09-thumb.png" data-width="2067" data-height="1032"></a><div class="caption"><p id="d1e2285"><strong class="caption-number">FigureÂ 9</strong>Distribution of selected topographic characteristics of intermediate catchments and full catchments. Each category's histogram indicates the number of basins (out ofÂ 3254). In a histogram, the <span class="inline-formula"><i>x</i></span>Â axis represents the number of basins, while the <span class="inline-formula"><i>y</i></span>Â axis represents each subplot's caption. Circle sizes are proportional to catchment areas.</p></div><p class="downloads"></p></div>

      <p id="d1e2308">Despite the good performance of the algorithm, some limitations are worth noting. Uncertainties in the evaporation estimates are generally attributed
to three major sources, i.e., the input meteorological forcings, area estimations from Landsat images, and the limitations of the algorithms. As
reported by Zhao etÂ al. (2022), the uncertainty of reanalysis datasets isÂ 7.22â%, and the TerraClimate datasets used in this study produce the
most reliable evaporation estimates, resulting in a total uncertainty value ofÂ 9.93â%. Regarding reservoir water surface area, after applying the
algorithm developed by Donchyts etÂ al. (2022), these impacts can be reduced to a large extent. There is some room for improving evaporation rate
calculation, such as considering the effects of stratification on water temperature or including the advective heat fluxes from reservoir inflow,
outflow, and groundwater.</p>
</div>
</div><div class="sec"><h2 id="Ch1.S3.SS4"><span class="label">3.4</span>âRes-CN products for reservoir-catchment-level characteristics</h2>
  
<div class="sec"><h3 id="Ch1.S3.SS4.SSS1"><span class="label">3.4.1</span>âTopographic characteristics</h3>
  
      <p id="d1e2327">Nineteen topographic attributes are provided in Res-CN (TableÂ S10). The catchment area, slope, and catchment-level elevation (including mean, maximum,
minimum, and standard deviation values) were calculated based on MERIT Hydro and MERIT DEM (Yamazaki etÂ al., 2017, 2019). Slope was calculated using
the algorithm developed by Horn (1981). Moreover, we determine 10 indices of catchment shapes and stream network as they are vital in runoff
generation and flood situations. âmvert_angâ is defined as the angle between the longitudinal axis and the north direction, while âmvert_distâ is a
catchment's longitudinal axis distance. These two indices could determine the relative precipitation trajectory in combination with wind
speed. âelongation_ratioâ is a measure of roundness (i.e., the higher, the rounder) of the catchment and was calculated according to Subramanya
(2013). âstrm_densâ is often used for comparing catchments as it is a function of many catchment attributes such as climate, soil, and geology. We used
the MERITÂ Hydro database (Yamazaki etÂ al., 2019) to calculate stream density and length within a catchment. The form factor, shape factor, circulatory
ratio, and relief of each catchment are also provided. Besides these, we also added âresArearatioâ to describe the proportion of the reservoir water surface
area and storage to the catchment area (Fig.Â S14). High average catchment
elevations and slopes are most apparent in western China, which extend from the western YunnanâGuizhou Plateau and southern QinghaiâTibet Plateau to
the northwestern areas (Fig.Â 9a andÂ b). The streamflow density and resArearatio are relatively high in the central part of the Yangtze River basin, where
the artificial reservoirs are densely distributed (Fig.Â 9c andÂ d). High elongation ratios are widespread in China, and mvert_ang shows high values in
the south (Fig.Â 9e andÂ f).</p>

      <div class="fig" id="Ch1.F10"><a target="_blank" class="figure-link" href="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-f10-web.png"><img alt="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-f10" data-webversion="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-f10-web.png" src="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-f10-thumb.png" data-width="2067" data-height="1579"></a><div class="caption"><p id="d1e2332"><strong class="caption-number">FigureÂ 10</strong>Distribution of selected long-term mean meteorological variables and climate indices of intermediate catchments and full catchments. Each category's histogram indicates the number of basins (out ofÂ 3254). In a histogram, the <span class="inline-formula"><i>x</i></span>Â axis represents the number of basins, while the <span class="inline-formula"><i>y</i></span>Â axis represents each subplot's caption. Circle sizes are proportional to catchment areas.</p></div><p class="downloads"></p></div>

</div>
<div class="sec"><h3 id="Ch1.S3.SS4.SSS2"><span class="label">3.4.2</span>âClimatic characteristics</h3>
  
      <p id="d1e2363">Res-CN includes daily meteorological time series and 11Â attributes reflecting aspects of climatic characteristics (TablesÂ S11 andÂ S12). In this study,
we used the National Station-based Climatic DataÂ setÂ V3 (NSCD) to compute the catchment-level climatic characteristics at full catchments and
intermediate catchments. NSCD provides daily meteorological time series during 1951â2020 and has near 800Â stations in China, with the longest period
of gauged observations of precipitation, temperature, evaporation, wind speed, pressure, sunshine duration, relative humidity, and near-surface
temperature. The ground sites were sparse before 1970 (Hao etÂ al., 2021). To ensure data quality and to match the periods of other datasets in Res-CN, we
used the latter 41Â years (from 1980 to 2020) to generate a gridded dataset based on the inverse-distance-weighting interpolation technique
(Fig.Â A2). FigureÂ 10 shows the spatial distribution of selected long-term mean meteorological forcings of full catchments and intermediate
catchments. The precipitation and mean air temperature have an increasing trend from northwest to southeast. The sunshine durations are high in the
northern areas where latitude is high, and the maximum value isÂ 8.7â<span class="inline-formula">hâd<sup>â1</sup></span>. Wind speed is generally high in the northern mountainous areas, southwestern areas, and coastal
parts. The average evaporation is positively correlated with sunshine duration and is higher in the Yellow River basin and southwestern parts.</p>
      <p id="d1e2383">We calculated nine attributes for NSCD based on meteorological data between 1Â October 1990 and 30Â September 2020 to reflect aspects of climatic
characteristics. Using the Global Aridity Index and Potential Evapotranspiration Climate Database versionÂ 3 (Zomer et al.,
2022), we derived the reference evapotranspirationÂ (<span class="inline-formula">ET<sub>0</sub></span>) and aridity index. Aridity is
often calculated as a function of precipitation, temperature, andÂ <span class="inline-formula">ET<sub>0</sub></span>, and it quantifies the precipitation availability for atmospheric water
demand. Long-term daily precipitation, reference evapotranspiration, and aridity index (Fig.Â 10f) characterize the long-term climatic
characteristics. The seasonality of precipitation (Fig.Â 10g) and the fraction of precipitation falling as snow (not shown) are two attributes
characterizing seasonality, which yield the yearly maps of sinusoidal precipitation cycle. Short-term events (e.g., heavy rainfall and drought) are
characterized by the frequency and duration of heavy precipitation or dry days, as well as their most likely seasons of occurrence (Fig.Â 10h andÂ i). High
precipitation is most likely to occur in summer (Fig.Â 10h) forÂ 86â% of all 3254Â catchments, whereas dry days usually occur in winter (Fig.Â 10i)
forÂ 56â% of them.</p>
      <p id="d1e2408"><span id="page2795"></span>One key limitation of the NSCD is that these gauged stations are unevenly distributed across the nation and densely grouped in the eastern and middle
parts, which may affect the accuracy for some catchments. Nevertheless, the NSCD meteorological dataset has been widely used as the most reliable
observational reference by many studies (Gu etÂ al., 2022). It is produced from a larger number of gauged stations and followed by strict
quality-assurance procedures and consistence check. Its accuracy and completeness of each meteorological variable from 1951 to 2020 are significantly
improved compared with similar data products in China; the missing rate of data of each element is generally belowÂ 1â%, and the correct rate of
data is close toÂ 100â%. Relative humidity has a suspicious rate of 0.6â%, large evaporation has a suspicious rate of 1.2â%, and great wind has a suspicious
rate of 1.6â%; the suspicious rate of other elements does not exceed 1â/â1000 (Hao etÂ al., 2021). The error rate of sunshine hours is 1â/â10â000, and there are no wrong data for other meteorological variables.</p>
</div>
<div class="sec"><h3 id="Ch1.S3.SS4.SSS3"><span class="label">3.4.3</span>âLand cover characteristics</h3>
  
      <p id="d1e2419">We provide three vegetation indices (EVI, enhanced vegetation index; LAI, leaf area index; and NDVI, normalized difference vegetation index), two
indicators of land use (GPP, gross primary productivity; NPP, net primary production), two average rooting depths (50â% andÂ 99â%), and 10Â land
cover classes in each catchment (TableÂ S13). LAI was derived from the MODIS MCD15A3H dataset with a temporal resolution ofÂ 4â<span class="inline-formula">d</span> and a spatial
resolution ofÂ 500â<span class="inline-formula">m</span> (Myneni etÂ al., 2015) and used for characterizing vegetation growth. NDVI and EVI were derived from MODIS MOD13Q1
(Didan, 2021) and used to monitor and classify vegetation. These vegetation indices were computed as the maximum, minimum, or difference (e.g.,
<span class="inline-formula">LAI<sub>max</sub></span>, <span class="inline-formula">LAI<sub>min</sub></span>, and <span class="inline-formula">LAI<sub>diff</sub></span>). <span class="inline-formula">LAI<sub>max</sub></span> measures the maximum evaporative and vegetation interception capacity,
and <span class="inline-formula">LAI<sub>diff</sub></span> shows its temporal variations. GPP and NPP were derived from MODIS MOD17A2H (Running etÂ al., 2021) and MOD17A3HGF (Running and Zhao, 2021) datasets, respectively, and calculated
for the whole period of 1Â February 2000 to 1Â January 2022. Meanwhile, the time series of both vegetation indices and indicators of land use are also
provided in Res-CN. Rooting depths are important parameters to characterize the water holding capacity underground and annual evapotranspiration
of topsoil. We calculated two average rooting depths (i.e.,Â 50â% andÂ 99â%) based on the IGBP (International GeosphereâBiosphere Programme)
classification (Zeng, 2001).</p>
      <p id="d1e2494">Each catchment was described using 10 land cover classes based on ESA WorldCoverÂ 10â<span class="inline-formula">m</span> (Zanaga etÂ al., 2021). This dataset is a new baseline of
a global land cover product at 10â<span class="inline-formula">m</span>Â spatial resolution for 2020 in almost near real time based on both Sentinel-1 and Sentinel-2
data. Sentinel-1 can provide complementary information on the observed structural characteristics of land cover in areas where the Sentinel-2 images
were covered by clouds. Thus, the combination of Sentinel-1 and Sentinel-2 data enables mapping of land cover almost in real time. It includes 11Â land
cover classes and an<span id="page2796"></span> overall accuracy ofÂ 75â%, providing valuable information for food security, carbon assessment, biodiversity, and climate
modeling. The dominant class and fractions of each class were computed with the GEE platform.</p>
      <p id="d1e2513">Some limitations of these datasets are identified. First, misclassification of ESA WorldCover occurs in areas of irrigated agriculture and wetlands
due to the high similarity of their hyperspectral spectrum. Second, although Res-CN provide time series of vegetation indices, it should be noted that
NDVI often provides inaccurate measurements of vegetation density, the accuracy of which can only be guaranteed by long-term measurements. In
addition, NDVI cannot provide quantitative estimates of vegetation density, so other attributes (i.e., LAI) are provided as a complement.</p>

      <div class="fig" id="Ch1.F11"><a target="_blank" class="figure-link" href="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-f11-web.png"><img alt="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-f11" data-webversion="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-f11-web.png" src="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-f11-thumb.png" data-width="2067" data-height="1579"></a><div class="caption"><p id="d1e2519"><strong class="caption-number">FigureÂ 11</strong>Distribution of selected land cover characteristics of intermediate catchments and full catchments. Each category's histogram indicates the number of basins (out ofÂ 3254). In a histogram, the <span class="inline-formula"><i>x</i></span>Â axis represents the number of basins, while the <span class="inline-formula"><i>y</i></span>Â axis represents each subplot's caption. Circle sizes are proportional to catchment areas.</p></div><p class="downloads"></p></div>

      <p id="d1e2542"><span id="page2797"></span>Trees are prevalent across the nation, and grasslands and croplands are two other dominant land cover types in China. Grassland has higher coverage
largely in the Hai River basin and the Yellow River basin (Fig.Â 11c). Croplands are widespread in China, especially in the Yellow River, Yangtze
River, and Huai River basins with a low mean slope (Fig.Â 11d). Fraction of barren sparse vegetation is quite small across the nation
(Fig.Â 11e). Natural wetlands or waterbodies are mainly distributed in the Yangtze River basin and areas surrounding the Bohai Sea, and these waterbodies are mainly artificial reservoirs and natural lakes (Fig.Â 11f). Catchments with a relatively high fraction of snow and ice are mainly located in
the Tibet Plateau, which is the source region of large rivers in China (not shown). A small proportion of the catchment area is typically considered
âbuilt-upâ, andÂ 6â% of the catchments have impervious area greater thanÂ 0.05 (not shown). There is a spatial correlation between
<span class="inline-formula">LAI<sub>max</sub></span> and <span class="inline-formula">LAI<sub>diff</sub></span> with tree fraction (CCâ<span class="inline-formula">=</span>â0.74 and CCâ<span class="inline-formula">=</span>â0.60, respectively). <span class="inline-formula">LAI<sub>diff</sub></span> has values similar to
<span class="inline-formula">LAI<sub>max</sub></span> over most areas but should be smaller in areas with a high proportion of trees due to permanent green cover. A negative correlation is
evident between the <span class="inline-formula">NDVI<sub>max</sub></span> (Fig.Â 11i, CCâ<span class="inline-formula">=</span>â<span class="inline-formula">â</span>0.78) and the mean catchment elevation (Fig.Â 9c).</p>
</div>
<div class="sec"><h3 id="Ch1.S3.SS4.SSS4"><span class="label">3.4.4</span>âSoil and geology characteristics</h3>
  
      <p id="d1e2637">Res-CN provided 154Â attributes to characterize physical and chemical properties of soil (TableÂ S14). TheÂ pH inÂ <span class="inline-formula">H<sub>2</sub>O</span>, cation exchange capacity
(CEC), bulk density, and organic carbon content were determined from the SoilGrids250m dataset (SG250; Hengl etÂ al., 2017). The SoilGrids250m dataset
predicted soil properties at six different soil layers (i.e.,Â 0â0.05, 0.05â0.15, 0.15â0.3, 0.3â0.6, 0.6â1, and 1â2â<span class="inline-formula">m</span>) using machine
learning techniques, utilizing data from approximately 150â000 soil profiles and 158Â environmental covariates derived from remote sensing data on a
global scale. Data within Res-CN are provided for each soil layer and all soil layers using the depth-weighted averaging method. Soil pH is an important
variable as it controls many other soil biological, chemical, and physical properties. CEC defines the sum of exchangeable cations that soil can hold
and is therefore a measure of fertility and nutrient retention capacity. The density and organic carbon content refer to the mass of organic carbon per
unit volume and mass, respectively. Besides SG250, the dataset of soil hydraulic and thermal parameters produced by Dai etÂ al. (2019) was also
used. Dai etÂ al. (2019) generated six soil layers of global soil hydraulic and thermal parameters with four products of vertical profiles available
using multiple PTFs (pedotransfer functions) based on SG250 and a soil dataset from Shangguan etÂ al. (2014). We adopted the products with the vertical
resolutions of SG250 and computed soil characteristics (saturated water content, saturated hydraulic conductivity, and other thermal parameters) for
all soil layers. Additional attributes derived from Shangguan etÂ al. (2013) include clay, silt, sand, and rock fragment proportions; soil profile
depth; and soil organic carbon (SOC) content. With the use of the polygon linkage method, this database provides soil<span id="page2798"></span> physical and chemical attributes for
land surface modeling in China at a 30â<span class="inline-formula">arcsec</span>Â resolution. Proportions of clay, silt, and sand refer to the fractions of particlesÂ <span class="inline-formula">&lt;</span>â0.002â<span class="inline-formula">mm</span>, particles <span class="inline-formula">â¥</span>â0.002â<span class="inline-formula">mm</span> and <span class="inline-formula">â¤</span>â0.05â<span class="inline-formula">mm</span>, and particlesÂ <span class="inline-formula">&gt;</span>â0.05â<span class="inline-formula">mm</span>
andÂ <span class="inline-formula">&lt;</span>â2â<span class="inline-formula">mm</span> in the fraction of particles smaller thanÂ 2â<span class="inline-formula">mm</span>, respectively. SOCÂ is a key variable for ecosystems and affects moisture
regimes and ground thermal properties.</p>

      <div class="fig" id="Ch1.F12"><a target="_blank" class="figure-link" href="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-f12-web.png"><img alt="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-f12" data-webversion="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-f12-web.png" src="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-f12-thumb.png" data-width="2067" data-height="1567"></a><div class="caption"><p id="d1e2756"><strong class="caption-number">FigureÂ 12</strong>Distribution of selected soil and geology characteristics of intermediate catchments and full catchments. Each category's histogram indicates the number of basins (out ofÂ 3254). In a histogram, the <span class="inline-formula"><i>x</i></span>Â axis represents the number of basins, while the <span class="inline-formula"><i>y</i></span>Â axis represents each subplot's caption. Circle sizes are proportional to catchment areas.</p></div><p class="downloads"></p></div>

      <p id="d1e2779">Geology characteristics are described by 19Â attributes (i.e., subsurface porosity, permeability, and lithological classes) derived from global
lithological map (GLiM; Hartmann and Moosdorf, 2012) and global hydrogeology map (GLHYMPS; Gleeson etÂ al., 2014) datasets (TableÂ S15). The two
important parameters for groundwater modeling, e.g., subsurface porosity and permeability, came from GLHYMPS. Subsurface porosity is a measure of the
ability of the subsurface to store water, while permeability is a measure of the ability of the rock to transmit fluids. Both of these parameters
show a high spatial correlation with GLiM, as hydraulic properties in GLHYMPS are based primarily on lithological classes of GLiM. We computed the
catchment-level characteristics by applying an arithmetic mean method for porosity and arithmetic-scale geometric mean method for permeability. The
lithological classes were derived from GLiM, which was created by summarizing 92 regional lithological maps and offers three classification levels
of detail. In this study, we adopted the first level of GLiM, which has 16Â lithological classes. Proportions of each lithological class and the
dominant class are documented in our datasets.</p>
      <p id="d1e2783">There are some limitations with these datasets. Firstly, the GLHYMPS module is primarily useful for analyzing regional scales, i.e., scales larger
than 5â<span class="inline-formula">km</span>, where the effects of local heterogeneities such as fault zones are negligible (Gleeson etÂ al., 2014). Secondly, GLHYMPS is not
adequate for analysis in regions dominated by unsaturated processes such as deeply weathered soils, as it is modeled for saturated conditions
(Huscroft etÂ al., 2018). Thirdly, data quality varies depending on location, based on raw regional geological maps available in different resolutions
and data qualities. In this study, the resolution of the Chinese raw data sources is slightly lower than that of GLiM (Hao etÂ al., 2021).</p>
      <p id="d1e2794">The soil pHÂ value is high in northern and northeastern China, and the saturated water content is low in this area (Fig.Â 12a andÂ b). High values of
CEC can be found in central and northeastern China and forested parts on the QinghaiâTibet Plateau (Fig.Â 12c). The clay content is low in northern China, while the sand content shows the opposite pattern (Fig.Â 12d andÂ e). The silt content has a wide predominance pattern in China,
particularly in the middle and northeastern parts (Fig.Â 12f). Moreover, we found that soil characteristics are correlated with other attributes from
Res-CN as they are predicted by fusing multisource covariates such as climate and landscape attributes. For example, the soil texture (i.e., sand,
silt, and clay contents) shows a similar pattern to the aridity index (Figs.Â 12e andÂ 10f). SOC has a high correlation value with surface slope
(Figs.Â 12c andÂ 10b, <span class="inline-formula"><i>R</i><sup>2</sup></span>â<span class="inline-formula">=</span>â0.94). The geology attributes have a clear latitude distribution, and the main lithological classes include acid
plutonic rocks (21â% of the catchments), siliciclastic sedimentary (20â%), mixed sedimentary (19â%), and carbonate sedimentary
(17â%). Medium subsurface porosity and high permeability are typically in northern and coastal areas surrounding the Bohai Sea with abundant
unconsolidated sediments, and high porosity and low permeability are often associated with mixed sedimentary rocks in northern Inner Mongolia, the western
YunnanâGuizhou Plateau, and the southern QinghaiâTibet Plateau (Fig.Â 12h, i, andÂ e). Interestingly, high values of subsurface porosity are not
necessarily accompanied by high values of permeability, producing a more heterogeneous spatial pattern (Fig.Â 12h) than that in Fig.Â 12i. There may be
differences in permeability and porosity due to the different rock structures of GLiM (Gleeson etÂ al., 2014).</p>
</div>
<div class="sec"><h3 id="Ch1.S3.SS4.SSS5"><span class="label">3.4.5</span>âAnthropogenic activity characteristics</h3>
  
      <p id="d1e2824">We computed four categories of anthropogenic activities in the catchments, including population count, human footprint, nighttime lights, and road
density (TableÂ S16). Population counts were derived from the GPW v4.11 (Gridded Population of the World, Center for International Earth Science
Information NetworkÂ â CIESINÂ â Columbia University, 2018). This collection produces human population estimates for five years (i.e., 2000, 2005,
2010, 2015, and 2020) at a gridded resolution of 30â<span class="inline-formula">arcsec</span>. Human footprint is a measure of human activities that use natural resources on
Earth and was extracted from the Global Human Footprint dataset (Venter etÂ al., 2016). This database measures the cumulative environmental impact of
indirect and direct human activities in 1993 and 2009, and it was produced from eight inputs: electric infrastructure, population density, built
environments, pastures, croplands, roads, railroads, and navigable waterways. Nighttime lights are a measure of human activity intensity and were
derived from the DMSP-OLS Nighttime Lights version 4 (Defense Meteorological Program-Operational Line-Scan; Doll, 2008). This database can detect
visible and near-infrared emission sources at night and consists of cloud-free composites made using all the available archived DMSP-OLS smooth
resolution data at a resolution ofÂ 30â<span class="inline-formula">arcsec</span>. The âavg_lights_x_pcâ in this dataset is used in this study and represents the mean
value of cloud-free light detections in the visible band. The road density was obtained from the Global Roads Inventory Project (GRIP; Meijer etÂ al., 2018),
providing global raster datasets of road density at aÂ 5â<span class="inline-formula">arcmin</span> spatial resolution. Using 60Â geospatial datasets on road infrastructure, this
inventory gathers, harmonizes, and integrates over <span class="inline-formula">21Ã10<sup>6</sup></span>â<span class="inline-formula">km</span> of roads by country.</p>
      <p id="d1e2874">These datasets are subject to some limitations. First, cumulative pressures of human footprint are static through time due to a lack of available
data, which would lead to an underestimation of human footprint if those pressures expanded at an above-average rate. Second, some static<span id="page2799"></span> pressures,
like the pollution and invasive species, are not considered in the cumulative pressures of human footprint. Third, the GRIP datasets cannot quantify
historic road expansion due to the missing information on the year of construction. Fourth, DMSP-OLS has the blooming effect (i.e., overestimation of
lit area) due to the low spatial resolution and the reflectance of light from adjacent waterbodies.</p>

      <div class="fig" id="Ch1.F13"><a target="_blank" class="figure-link" href="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-f13-web.png"><img alt="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-f13" data-webversion="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-f13-web.png" src="https://essd.copernicus.org/articles/15/2781/2023/essd-15-2781-2023-f13-thumb.png" data-width="2067" data-height="1567"></a><div class="caption"><p id="d1e2879"><strong class="caption-number">FigureÂ 13</strong>Distribution of human activity characteristics of intermediate and full catchments. Each category's histogram indicates the number of basins (out ofÂ 3254). In a histogram, the <span class="inline-formula"><i>x</i></span>Â axis represents the number of basins, while the <span class="inline-formula"><i>y</i></span>Â axis represents each subplot's caption. Circle sizes are proportional to catchment areas.</p></div><p class="downloads"></p></div>

      <p id="d1e2903">FigureÂ 13 illustrates the spatial distribution of four anthropogenic indices in the catchments. Road density, population count, lights, and human
footprint show similar patterns, suggesting that intense human activities are distributed in the coastal lines surrounding the East Sea and Bohai Sea,
middle China, and northeastern China, and there is almost no human activity in northwestern China due to high elevation and harsh environment.</p>
</div>
</div></div><span class="section3-mobile-bottom-border mobile-bottom-border hide-on-desktop hide-on-tablet"></span></div>
                <div class="sec" id="section4"><div class="grid-container no-margin header-element"><span class="grid-100 mobile-grid-100 tablet-grid-100 grid-parent more-less-mobile" data-hide="#section4 .co-arrow-open,.section4-content" data-show="#section4 .co-arrow-closed,.section4-mobile-bottom-border"><div id="Ch1.S4" class="h1"><span class="label">4</span>âData availability<span class="hide-on-desktop hide-on-tablet triangleWrapper"> <i class="co-arrow-closed"></i><i class="co-arrow-open" style="display:none"></i></span></div></span></div>
  
      
<div class="section4-content show-no-js hide-on-mobile-soft"><p id="d1e2916">The Res-CN archive can be found here: <a href="https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.7664489">https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.7664489</a> (Shen etÂ al., 2022c). It is distributed with a CC-BY license. The files provided are (A)Â shapefiles; (B)Â full catchments containing all catchment-level attributes such as climate, topographic, land cover, soil, geological, anthropogenic activities, and time series of meteorological variables; (C)Â intermediate catchments; (D)Â reservoir states (i.e., water level, water surface area, evaporation, and storage anomaly); and (E)Â an information file in English containing more information on RES-CN, as well as validation figures containing the figures depicting the time series of reservoir states and detailed evaluation reports based on in situ data when available. All input datasets are summarized in the Supplement and kindly acknowledged.</p></div><span class="section4-mobile-bottom-border mobile-bottom-border hide-on-desktop hide-on-tablet"></span></div>
                <div class="sec" id="section5"><div class="grid-container no-margin header-element"><span class="grid-100 mobile-grid-100 tablet-grid-100 grid-parent more-less-mobile" data-hide="#section5 .co-arrow-open,.section5-content" data-show="#section5 .co-arrow-closed,.section5-mobile-bottom-border"><div id="Ch1.S5" class="h1"><span class="label">5</span>âCode availability<span class="hide-on-desktop hide-on-tablet triangleWrapper"> <i class="co-arrow-closed"></i><i class="co-arrow-open" style="display:none"></i></span></div></span></div>
  
      
<div class="section5-content show-no-js hide-on-mobile-soft"><p id="d1e2932">All scripts for generating our reservoir datasets are available in the data product.</p></div><span class="section5-mobile-bottom-border mobile-bottom-border hide-on-desktop hide-on-tablet"></span></div>
                <div class="sec conclusions" id="section6"><div class="grid-container no-margin header-element"><span class="grid-100 mobile-grid-100 tablet-grid-100 grid-parent more-less-mobile" data-hide="#section6 .co-arrow-open,.section6-content" data-show="#section6 .co-arrow-closed,.section6-mobile-bottom-border"><div id="Ch1.S6" class="h1"><span class="label">6</span>âSummary, applications, and outlook<span class="hide-on-desktop hide-on-tablet triangleWrapper"> <i class="co-arrow-closed"></i><i class="co-arrow-open" style="display:none"></i></span></div></span></div>
  
      
      
      
<div class="section6-content show-no-js hide-on-mobile-soft"><p id="d1e2943">In this study, we have produced a comprehensive and extensive dataset of reservoir-catchment characteristics in China, Res-CN, which complementarily
improved the existing reservoir datasets. We demonstrated that the construction of Res-CN involved a first known effort to construct catchment-level
characteristics of reservoirs for our delineated full catchments and intermediate catchments of reservoirs. The 512Â static catchment-level attributes for
3254 reservoirs in six categories (i.e., reservoir and catchment body characteristics, topography, climate, soil and geology, land cover and use, and
anthropogenic activity characteristics) are included in Res-CN. Additionally, 15Â climatic variables were extracted at daily scale, which can drive
machine learning models or hydrological models for simulations. Alongside the catchment-level attributes, we produced a significantly enhanced spatial
and temporal coverage (e.g., 67â%Â increase in spatial resolution of water level and 225â%Â increase in storage anomaly) of water level (data
available forÂ 20â% of 3254Â reservoirs), water surface area (99â%), storage anomaly (92â%), and evaporation (98â%) by utilizing
multiple satellites such as operational satellite altimeters and imagery data. In situ data of 138Â reservoirs are employed in this study as a valuable
reference for evaluation, thus enhancing our confidence in the data quality and enhancing our understanding of the accuracy of current satellite
datasets. We have considered and discussed the deficits, limitations, and uncertainties of Res-CN for further applications.</p><p id="d1e2946">We envision that Res-CN with its comprehensive and extensive attributes can provide strong support to a wide range<span id="page2801"></span> of applications and
disciplines. Firstly, our two types of catchments along with their catchment-level attributes allow for investigations within individual catchments and
interconnected river networks. For example, as illustrated in Fig.Â 2, users may quantify the relative contributions of upstream reservoirs and local
drainage catchment on water quality (e.g., algae contributions and water color) of the downstream reservoir by tracking temperature and nutrient flows
from upstream reservoirs and intermediate catchments (e.g., X.Â Hou etÂ al., 2022; Yang etÂ al., 2022). Besides, water and sediment transfer can also be more accurately simulated in
such a spatially explicit context if appropriate approaches are used. Machine learning methods make it possible to predict reservoir storage change at a
1- to 3-month lead from reservoir upstream attributes and timeÂ series of reservoir states (Tiwari and Mishra, 2019). Secondly, Res-CN provides thus far the most comprehensive reservoir states in China for assessing impacts of reservoir regulation
and dynamics. Tracking the spatiotemporal balance of reservoir evaporative and water storage can provide a basis for local water management in a
warming climate (DiÂ Baldassarre etÂ al., 2019). The reservoir operational rules or impacts of reservoir regulation on flow regimes are possibly
inferred from reservoir water dynamics in Res-CN (VuÂ etÂ al., 2022). This is particularly true if the reservoir inflow is also utilized. Recently, the
gridded natural runoff provided by Gou etÂ al. (2021) provides exciting opportunities for quantifying human water regulation in combination with Res-CN (Dang etÂ al., 2022; Shin etÂ al., 2020). Thirdly, our extracted catchment-level attributes can
contribute to a better understanding of reservoir water amount and water quality changes by spatially incorporating geophysical and anthropogenic
characteristics of their upstream catchments and their respective contributions. For example, cropland in reservoir upstream catchments controls the
nutrient-driven primary production, while wetland coverage affects dissolved organic material transport downstream, ultimately impacting primary
production and <span class="inline-formula">CO<sub>2</sub></span>Â emissions in lakes (Balmer and Downing, 2011; Borges etÂ al., 2022; Maberly etÂ al., 2013). Gradient and altitude in the
reservoir geological attributes may affect greenhouse gas emissions and biogeochemistry of a reservoir (Casas-Ruiz etÂ al., 2020). Furthermore, these
catchment-level attributes can be used to explore water fluxes and sediment transportation even in reservoirs that have not been sampled. Studies on
cascading patterns in reservoir attributes found that each attribute may display a linear function of catchment area, concluding that cascading patterns
of each attribute have different implications for dam management (Faucheux etÂ al., 2022). For instance, one study combined knowledge of catchment
attributes with economic, climate, and landscape data to inform reservoir removal decisions in California's Central Valley basin (Null etÂ al.,
2014). Lastly, carbon dioxide emissions from reservoirs show significant spatial and seasonal variation, highlighting the importance of hydrology in
terrestrialâreservoir carbon transfers and the need to consider this effect when plumbing terrestrial carbon budgets. Res-CN also offers exciting
opportunities to address changes in reservoir storage that may be linked to carbon dioxide emissions changes.</p><p id="d1e2960">Although Res-CN presents significant improvements over existing datasets and holds potential for various applications identified above, a few
limitations should be acknowledged. Res-CN is generated using GeoDAR v1 shapefiles (Wang etÂ al., 2022) instead of the newly produced datasets by Song
etÂ al. (2022), which added an additional nearly 60â000 very small reservoir shapefiles (<span class="inline-formula">&lt;</span>â1â<span class="inline-formula">km<sup>2</sup></span>). As this study aims to provide a
comprehensive and extensive dataset of reservoir-catchment characteristics in China for a better understanding of reservoir impacts on hydrological
and biochemical cycles, these thousands of very small reservoirs are not included in our study. Meanwhile, it is currently not feasible to generate
satellite-based datasets for these small reservoirs due to the limitations of current satellite altimetry missions, which are unable to detect such
reservoirs because of the sparsity of their altimetric ground tracks. These additional small reservoirs only account for 8â% of total water
capacity in China. Nonetheless, users can freely access our codes to calculate any reservoir attribute for individual applications or other areas, and they
can enrich the inventory if new data become available.</p></div><span class="section6-mobile-bottom-border mobile-bottom-border hide-on-desktop hide-on-tablet"></span></div>
                <div class="app sec" id="section7">
  <div class="grid-container no-margin header-element"><span class="grid-100 mobile-grid-100 tablet-grid-100 grid-parent more-less-mobile" data-hide="#section7 .co-arrow-open,.section7-content" data-show="#section7 .co-arrow-closed,.section7-mobile-bottom-border"><div id="App1.Ch1.S1" class="h1"><span class="section-number"></span>Appendix A<span class="hide-on-desktop hide-on-tablet triangleWrapper"> <i class="co-arrow-closed"></i><i class="co-arrow-open" style="display:none"></i></span></div></span></div>

                <div id="section8" class="sec">
  <div class="grid-container no-margin header-element"><span class="grid-100 mobile-grid-100 tablet-grid-100 grid-parent more-less-mobile" data-hide="#section8 .co-arrow-open,.section8-content" data-show="#section8 .co-arrow-closed,.section8-mobile-bottom-border"><div class="h1"><span class="section-number"> </span>Supplement<span class="hide-on-desktop hide-on-tablet triangleWrapper"> <i class="co-arrow-closed"></i><i class="co-arrow-open" style="display:none"></i></span></div></span></div>
  
<div class="section8-content show-no-js hide-on-mobile-soft"><p id="d1e3031">The Supplement contains many tables, figures, and texts illustrating the methodologies and source datasets for generating Res-CN, as well as detailed evaluations of our Res-CN datasets and all necessary explanations related to this article. The supplement related to this article is available online at:Â <a href="https://doi.org/10.5194/essd-15-2781-2023-supplement">https://doi.org/10.5194/essd-15-2781-2023-supplement</a>.</p></div><span class="section8-mobile-bottom-border mobile-bottom-border hide-on-desktop hide-on-tablet"></span></div>
                <div id="section9" class="sec"><div class="grid-container no-margin header-element"><span class="grid-100 mobile-grid-100 tablet-grid-100 grid-parent more-less-mobile" data-hide="#section9 .co-arrow-open,.section9-content" data-show="#section9 .co-arrow-closed,.section9-mobile-bottom-border"><div class="h1"><span class="section-number"> </span>Author contributions<span class="hide-on-desktop hide-on-tablet triangleWrapper"> <i class="co-arrow-closed"></i><i class="co-arrow-open" style="display:none"></i></span></div></span></div>

                        <div class="widget dark-border">
                            <div class="legend journal-contentLinkColor">Short summary</div>
                            <div class="content hide-js shortSummaryFull">Res-CN fills a gap in a comprehensive and extensive dataset of reservoir-catchment characteristics for 3254 Chinese reservoirs with 512 catchment-level attributes and significantly enhanced spatial and temporal coverage (e.g., 67&thinsp;% increase in water level and 225&thinsp;% in storage anomaly) of time series of reservoir water level (data available for 20&thinsp;% of 3254 reservoirs), water area (99&thinsp;%), storage anomaly (92&thinsp;%), and evaporation (98&thinsp;%), supporting a wide range of applications and disciplines.</div>
                            <div style="display: none" class="content show-js shortSummaryShorten">Res-CN fills a gap in a comprehensive and extensive dataset of reservoir-catchment...</div>

                                <a href="#" class="more-less show-js triangle" data-hide=".shortSummaryFull" data-show=".shortSummaryShorten" data-toggleCaption='Hide'>Read more</a>
